1. The Palazzo Bevilacqua, Verona

Has rustications carrying across the pilasters on the ground storey and spirally-fluted corinthian attached columns on the upper tier, arranged in alternating spacings suggestive of the Ro""'an triumphal arch' motif. These features of the upper floor and the smaller arched openings capped by alternating triangular and segmental headed pediments, all have direct precedents in Roman arches, but the sculptured Spandrels and Keystones are his own innovations.

- *triangular, segmental and broken pediments any roof end.
- *spandrel - The triangular space enclosed by the curve of an arch, a vertical line from its springing, and a horizontal line through its apex.

2. The Palazzo Pomepeii, Verona, is a stately composition on axial lines; with a simple arched portal leading to a cortile. A rusticated basement with arched windows, supports the piano nobile, with its fluted Doric columns, taiiPsemi-circular-headed dows and carved masky on keystone. ·

- 3. The Pal.azzo Grimani, Venice, facing the grand canal, is San Michelle's greatest work. The plan is most cleverly contrived on an irregular island site with three large openings to the columned vestibule and long hall, off which are the staircase. The symmetrical facade, 27.5 m (90. ft.) long and 29.5 m (97ft.) high, has superimposed corinthian orders the lower comprising two storeys and the whole bound together with a striking balcony stretching from end to end. By doubling the order to demarcate the end windows, the customary Venetian fenestr-ation is preserved.

- 4. The Basilica, Vicenza, is famous for its Renaissance arcades added by Palladdio. The design was won "in competition in 1545 and completed in 1614. The end bays on each facade were unrestricted in width, so palladia made them narrower in order to give an effect of strength at the angles. These arcades, in time hard stone which, under the main entablature; frame intervening arches supported on smaller freestanding twin columns, and there are circular openings in the spandrels. This grouping


combination of columns and arches has been termed the "Palladian Motif", and is exceedingly effective especially when seen in coniunction with the slender campanile alongside.

###### C. BAROQUE

1. Sta. Maria della Salute. Venice

The church is in form, with a central space, ft.) in diameter, with corinthian columns in the angles, and the spacious surrounding ambulatory and radiating chapels make it one of the largest aisled, polygonal churches. The circular dome with high drum is connected to the outer walls by scrolled buttress which contribute much to the effect. The second dome with its flanking turrets over the wide chacel adds to the -picturesqueness of this majestic group, which throned upon its measured steps above canal, is the apotheosis of the Baroque style in Venice.

###### .1n

###### RENAISSANCE ARCHITECTURE FRANCE

###### 15th-19th Century

###### INFLUENCES

###### GEOGRAPHICAL

France had become one Kingdom, with Paris as its centre, from which the new Renaissance influence radiated to all parts of the country. This new geographical condition conduced to a homogenous development within her extended boundaries. The distance of Paris from the centre of the Renaissance movement in Italy helped to delay its adoption in France but Italian influences, long powerful in the Southern commercial capital of lyons, grew rapidly from 1500.

###### FRANCE IN THE SIXTEENTH CENTURY

![image 771](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile771.png)

###### GEOLOGICAL

Throughout France there was good building stone, easily worked; so much so that Paris, is a city of stone. London is a city of Brick. Iron. wrought and cast, came into use as a building materials shortly after 1780.

###### CLIMATIC

The climate asserted its influence on architecture in demanding a continuance of large windows, high-pitched roofs and lofty chimneys, which differentiated Renaissanc architecture in France from that in Italy.

###### HISTORICAL, SOCIAL AND RELIGIOUS

The chief factor in the process of building up the Kingdom of France was the struggle to expel the English. inspired by Joan of Arc's leadership (1429-31) and culminating in the expulsion of the English in 1453. A new national feeling was then created. which as in othe·r countries, gave a great impetus to architecture, and resulted in the erection of many fine buildings, which have been held worthy to rank as national monuments.

During the first half of the 16th century, Italy became the battlefield of Europe. In these wars the trench Kings, while failing in their actual object. were brought into contact with the older civilization of Italy and were thus drawn into the Renaissance movement. France then achieved an unprecedented unity, power and splendour. establishing a prestige in Europe which was riot secured without civil and religious tribulations and military and diplomatic clashes with her neighbours. Napoleon Bonaparte emerged as an omnipotent national figure establishing an empire (1804-14) by feats of arms but lost at the hands of Wellington at waterloo in 1815.

Paris. as the capital of the newly consolidated Kingdom of France and as the centre of the brilliant eourt of Francis I, attained Pre-eminence in art and literature. This resulted in the adoption of one national architectural style which emanated from Paris, and the schools in the vicinity; while the valley of the Loire became a highways along which. in response to new social conditions, the famous chateaux of kings and courties sprang up and formed models for other parts of the country.

In South-western France and other limited areas the Huguenot gentry and cities attained considerable strength during the prolonged wars of Religion (1562-98). Throughout most of France the supremacy of the Catholic church remained unshaken until the revolution. Many of the Huguenot craftsmen were driven abroad by persecution. In the 17th century, ·the Jesuit order-the chief influence on ecclesiastical architecture in France, built great churches designed for preaching to large congregations. with the obJect of refuting Reformation heresies.

449

![image 772](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile772.png)

###### ARCHITECTURAL CHARACTER

The Renaissance style in France took 75 years to take root than Italy. It may be divided into three periods:

1. The EARLY PERIOD-{1494-15891 or 16th century.

The special character of this transitional period lies in the combination of Gothic and Renaissance features to form a picturesque ensemble, while in Italy, a,return to classic forms took place, In France there was a period of transition, during which Renaissance details were grafted on to such Gothic features as flying buttresses and pinnacles.

St. Eustache, Paris planned like a five-aisled mediaeval church with apsidal end, high roofs, window tracery flying buttress, pinnacles and deeply-recessed portals, all clothed with Renaissance details.

In Italy the principal buildings were erected in towns as palaces for Popes, prelates and nobles; while the principal buildings in France were castles in the country round Paris and on the loire for the King and his

450

![image 773](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile773.png)

Moreover, in Italy the influence of ancient Rome is apparent in the classical treatment of detail and ornament, while the influence of traditional Gothic craftsmanship was more pronounced in Franpe.

Then too, in Italy the predominant characteristics are stateliness and a tendency to classical horizontally, but in France the salient features are picturesqueness and a tendency to Gothic verticality. Early buildings of the period in Italy were principally churches, while in France the chateaux for the nobility are the early buildings are sufficient churches of the middle ages already existed.

- *chateaux - a castle or imposing country residence of nobility in old France. Now, any French country estate.

2. The CLASSICAL Period (1589-1715) or 17th century

The period is notable for the dignity, sobriety and masculine quality of its foremost buildings, resulting from the subordination of plan, composition an.d detail of the unity of the whole, and the charity and simplicity with which the elements were used. Ornament, though somewhat coarse, is vigorous and reasonably restrained.

In the earlier part of the period brick is much favoured as a building material, usually in conjunction with stone or stuccco used for 'quoins' and dressings and for 'chaines' which in lieu of pilasters, rise vertically between the string-mouldings and cornice so as to form wall-panels; These often having central framed ornaments or niches or being infilled with patterned brickwork.

- *quoins - in masonry a hard stone or brick used, with similar ones, to reinforce an external corner or edge of a wall or the like; often distinguished decoratively from adjacent masonry; may be imitated in non-load bearing materials.


•chaines - (chain) vertical strips of r!JSticated masonry rising between the horizontal string-mouldings and cornice of a building, and so dividing the facades into bays or panels.

451

![image 774](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile774.png)

Windows grew increasingly large, and ride up into the steep roofs as dormers, while stone mullions and transoms tend to give place to wood. There is much play with rustication, on the Orders themselves when these appear; sometimes the orders enframe dormers, as well as the windows aligned vertically below. Roofs at "first mostly are steep and treated in separate pavilion units, and the 'Mansard' roof of two different slopes is popular, but as the period develops, unified pitched roofs or flat roofs become increasingly common.

- *Dormer - a window in a sloping roof usually that of a sleeping-apartment.
- *Mullion - vertical members dividing windows into different numbers of lights.
- *transom - the horizontal divisions or crossbars of windows.
- *Mansard - a roof having a double slope on all four sides; the lower slope being much steeper, and flatter upper portion named after mansart. Also known as a gambrel roof.


452

![image 775](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile775.png)

The Orders figure much more frequently in the second half of the period, normally superimposed in the typical French manner, but with a little recourse to the giant order. The orders become much more strictly classical in proportions and detail than formerly, and this relatively simplicity of exterior design accentuates the contrast with interior decoration, which is brilliantly profuse in fanciful scrolls, nymphs. wreaths, and shells carried cut in stucco and 'papier mache'. forms of ornament also consistently applied to furniture and fittings. This was the great age of Renaissance architecture in France.

- *scroll - an ornamept consisting of aspirally wound band, either as a running ornament or as a. terminal, like the volutes of the ionic capital or the scrolls on consoles and modillions.
- *nymphaeum - a room decorated with plants, sculpture and fountains (often decorated with nymphs) and intended for relaxation.
- *nymphs - any group of minor nature goddesses represented as beautiful maidens living in rivers, mountains and trees.
- *wreath - a twisted band,. garland, or ch'ap!et, representing flowers, fruits, leaves often used in decoration.


453

![image 776](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile776.png)

- *papier-mache - a material composed principally of paper; usualy prepared by pulping a mass of paper (sometimes glue is added) to a dough-like consistently and molding to a desired form.

3. The Late Period - 18th century Architectually, three stylistic phases may be distinguished.

- 1. sovereign Louis XV
- 2. sovereign Louis XVI
- 3. Empire - 1790-1830


In the first phase there is a descent from the classical grandeur of the previous, Louis XIV, era, towards a relative intimacy of effect, particularly marked in domestic planning and in interior decoration. Very many modest residences and town 'hotels' were erected in which comfort and convenience were considered far more important than chilly dignity. Rooms were planned for independent approach rather than in sequence, now being interlocked in compact arrangements with many devices of circular. oval. curvilinear or polygonal shape to facilitate compression and produce diverting visual effects.

Double-depth or deep, squarish plans became normal. Internal corners of apartments sometimes were rounded, and occasionally walls followed sinvous curves on plan, this type of planning being the special forte of J.A. Meissonier, who at the same time contributed much to the development ot the exuberant style of decoration later to be known as 'Rococo', but in France generally described as 'Rocaille'.

- *Rococo - (Fr. Rocalle = rockwork} a term applied to a type of Renaissance ornament in which rock-like forms, fantastic scrolls, and crimped shells are worked up together in a profusion and confusion of detail often without organic coherence, but presenting a lavish display of decoration.


![image 777](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile777.png)

Externally, however, except in church architecture, architecture became more simple but at the same time less classically pure, the Orders often being substituted by Pilaster, 'chaines' of rustication and sparse ornament in domestic buildings, while windows grew larger still, often absorbing the greater part of the wall.

About the middle of the 18th century a romantic tendency made itself felt, leading to a return to the sober classicism of louis XIV's time and, more importantly, to a growing respect for the monuments of antiquity, stimulated by discoveries at Herculaneum (1719) and Pompeii (1748) and other cities in Italy, Asia minor & Greece. Measured drawings and 'restorations' of ancient remains appeared in increasing volume. The puristic reaction was anti-Baroque and anti-Rococo, yet was more effective in cleansing exterior architecture of superiluities than in materially reducing the richness of interiors, which became austere and refined rather than simple, while new decorative motifs were drawn from widely divergent sources; from the art of neighbouring countries, the orient, Egypt or from France's own antecedents as well as from classical antiquity.

Classicism, however chiefly gained the day in the 'Empire' phase. when a rigid formality was the keynote, Greece-Roman coalescing with Egyptian motifs to produce a distinctively French national decoratively style. Externally it was Roman character that was chiefly favoured, only slightly tinctured with the Greek; in France the Greek and the

,Gothic revivals never achieved the popularity that they did in the early 19th century England. Constructively, cast and wrought iron were exploited from the late 18th century, a dome and bridges having been erected in the material before 1810.

*Empire style - The elaborate neoclassic style of the French first Empire (1804-1815).

###### RENAISSANCE ARCHITECTURE 1n SPAIN and PORTUGAL

###### 16th-19th Century

###### INFLUENCES

###### GEOGRAPHICAL

In the Renaissance period, with the vast hereditary possesions and military conquests of the Spanish monarchy, and her power greatly enhanced by the discovery of America, Spain was the leading country of Europe, Her dominions embraced the NetMrtands and much of Central Europe, but after the Peace of Westphalia U6681 her prestige declmed . There remained, however, the colonies in America-Mexico, Peru, Ch1le and for Portugal, Brazil.

![image 826](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile826.png)

###### GEOLOGICAL

Granite was the principal material. Its hard severe nature ·had much to do w1th the grim aspect of the Escorial. Stone and semi-marbles also abounded and were m general use. .Brick was employed with stone in bonding courses. mainly 1n former centres such as Toledo, and in the iron ore of the Northern mountams gave an 1mpctus to the development of 'Rajas' decorative wrought-iron screens. In Portugal <:t grey-green gran1te produced most sympathetic effects especially in associat1on w1th stucco.

"'Reja -- an ornate iron grille or screen, a characteristic feature of Spanish church interiors.

![image 827](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile827.png)

###### CLIMATIC

The climate vanes trom severe wmter cold on the h1g11central table-lands to trop1ca1 summer heat in the south and, ow1ng to me gen1:1ra1 sunny character of the Peninsula, there IS a prevalence of smoll windows, flat roots and open'patios', or courtvards. The effects a1med at by the Baroque style wew particularly adapted to the clear a1r, rntense sun11ght and strong shadows of Spain and manv parts of Amenca .

*patio - a Spanish arcaded or colonnaded court-

yard.

###### HISTORICAL, SOCIAL AND RELIGIOUS

The power of Spain increased in the 15th century and became the chief power in Europe. In 1487 Diaz discovered the Cape of Good Hope; in 1492 Columbus d1scovered the West Indies. and in 1497 the Continent of Amenca. bringing consequent nches to Spain.

The marriage in 1469 between Ferdinand of Aragon and Isabella of caStile began that fuSIOn of the different States which resulted 1n the consolidation ot the k1ngdom ot Spain. In 1512 Ferdinand conquered the krngdom of Navarre. which was mcorporated with and thus the whole of was JOined under one rule and dunng the annexatton of Portugal (158 1640) the Spanish monarchy ruled the wt1ole peninsula.

The Protestant Reformat1on obtained few adherents m Spa1n. tor the rel1g1ous and racial struggle betweon chnstianity and Islam tormed a bond of un1on amongst all christian. The final conquest of the Moors (1492) resulted 1n a revival of ecclesiastical bu1ld1ng. and many fine Renaissance churches were erected in the hitherto marish districts. Spa1n was the country of Ignatius Loyola, the founder of the soc1ety of Jesus. and was responsible tor many magnificent Baroque chur · throughout the country.

###### ARCHITECTURAL CHARACTER

###### SPAIN

Renaissance Spain was heir to two civilizations. Moslem and Christian and is divided into tour phases:

1. The Early Period (1492-1556) is notable for the grafting of Renaissance details on to Gothic fqrms. and was influenced by the exuberant fancy ot Moorish art. Thus there had been produced by this time a style as rich and poet1c as anv in Europe, commonly known as the 'Piateresque' (plateria =- silverworkl, from the minuteness of its detail and its similarity to silversmiths' work which itself had received a great impetus through the importation of precious metals from the new world. The plastersque is extremely florid and decoratively involved, and has its fundamentally Gothic versions,.carrying on until mid-sixteenth century.

"p!ateresque - a phase of the Early period of Spanish architecture of the later 15th and ear.ly '16th .century, an intricate style named after its likeness to silver work.

477

![image 828](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile828.png)

- 2. The CLASSICAL PERIOD (1556-1690) was marked by a closer adherence to Italian Renaissance art.
- 3. The Baroque Period (1650-1750) was characterized by a reaction from the correct and frigid formalism observed by Herrera and his followers. There was no single version to which all designers of the day adhered. Classical rules was disregarded. The earlier Baroque show some relationship to central Italy and a fantastically extravagant expression, the 'churrigueresque' developed in the late seventh century and continued to micj-eighteenth; due to a family of architects led by Jose de Churrigvera (1650-1723). The Spanish Churrigveresque is well seen in the cathedral of Santiago de Compostela (1738-49).


- *churrlgueresque - an expression of Spanish Baroque architecture and sculpture associated with the churringuera family of artists and architects, but not inharmonious, decorative exuberance. In architecture a recurrent feature was the richly garlanded spiral column.

4. The ANTIQUARIAN PERIOD (1750-1830) architecture turned more and more towards

ancient classical models at this time.

PORTUGAL

The 'Manueline style', a peculiarly Portuguese phenomenon, was contemporary with the 'Early period' of the Spanish Renaissance. Taking its name from King Manuel I, who reigned from (1495-1521 ), it is decorative rather than structural in character and be.cause it was generally superimposed upon Gothic forms - The great monasteries of Belem and Batallia are notable examples. It is often classified as mediaeval.

- *Manueline architecture - The last phase of Gothic architecture in Portugal, So named afrer King Manuel I (1521).


478

![image 829](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile829.png)

Manueline drew its exuberant inspiration from the voyages of the discovers, exploiting in fantastic patterns, the symbols of the armillary sphere, ropes, corals and the cross of the order of christ, which Vasco da Gamma bore on the sails of ships. Apart from the Manueline, Portuguese architecture showed few distinctive characteristics during the Renaissance period until the splendid phrase of Baroque and Rococo in the first half of the 18th century, when sudden deriving from the discovery of gold and diamonds in Brazil led to a spate of building.

![image 830](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile830.png)

In 1755 occured the appalling disaster of the Lisbon earthquake, and from the rubble of destruction emerged some fine town planning. The style of the rehabilitated capital, 1n Baxia with the regular g,rid of the street plan, and the plain, nearly uniform, facades and standardized building elements, is sometimes called 'Pombaline', after the Marquis of Pombal, who directed the reconstruction program.

![image 831](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile831.png)

###### EXAMPLES A. SECULAR ARCHITECTURE

1. The Casa de las Conchas, Salamanca (1512-14)

Takes its name from the curious treatment of its facade, which is covered with carved Scallop Shells. The windows are few in number; the small lower ones are guarded with grilles of elaborate ironwork, while the upper ones have carved panels in lieu of balconies and are enriched with heraldic carvinas.

Is a masterpiece of Plateresque design of admirable craftsmanship and embodying, within a Gothic frame, a number of ltalianate motifs such as amorini, panelled pilasters infilled with arabesques, portrait roundels and candelabria as well as the arms of Ferdinand and Isabella and of Charles V, all embedded in awealth of surface ornament of Moorish inspiration.

*candelabra - candelabrum - a · movable. candle lampstand with central shat, and, often, branches or a decorative representation thereof.

3. The university facade, (1537-53)

Has the characteristically ornate centrepiece and pavcity of windows, except for an arcaded third storey windows series bracketing the greater part of the front. The main windows, on the first _floor, have side scrolls, excessive f;i!nframements and iron grilles, while the angle treatment and pinnacles strike a Gothic note.

The central entrance was flanked by Ionic columns surmounted by .statues, and the elaborate overdoor had a panel carved with the arms of Cha·rles·v. The first storey windows of the facade, with iron balconies, were set off by plain wallin, V\fhile the top storey had an unusual rusticated treatment with a small order on pedestals, surmounted by a flat balustraded roof.

###### RENAISSANCE ARCHITECTURE; BRITAIN

###### 16th-19th Century

###### INFLUENCES

###### GEOGRAPHICAL

Owing to the distance from Italy, the birthplace of the England was the last country to fall under the influence of the movement. The friendly relations which, at different times, marked its intercourse with these count-ries may be seen faithfully reflected in English architecture. The great wars. however, at the end of the 18th and beginning of 19th century closed continental travels to Englishmen. The first step as regards good road-making have been taken by General George Wade in Scotland from 1726; Bridge building necessarily improved. The peak period of canal and navigable inland waterway construction was between 1760 and 1800, and the extensive system created greatly facilitated the transport of heavy goods, including building materials, with the result that the surviving local charactefin minor architecture tended to disappear.

###### ENGLAND IN TH.E RENAISSANCE PERIOD

486

![image 841](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile841.png)

###### GEOLOGICAL

Timber fell into disuse for building purposes, partly because of the growing scarcity of thEl! material as forest were cleared for the needs of the rising population, or because of the risk of fire in crowded towns, but particularly because stone, and in due course, brick provided more stable, permanent and weather-proof structures. Timber-frames, exposed externally, were still normal, and under various forms of external sheathing, such as plasfer or tilehanging, persisted in the smaller buildings of the countryside even until the opening of the 19th century in regions where building-stone was not readily available.

"sheathing - The covering usually of wood boards or plywood. placed exterior studding or rafters of a building; provides a base for the application of wall or roof cladding.

Stone was the natural successor to timber and HJ igo Jones first made use of portland stone in his London buildigns. Sir Christopher Wren also adopted stone for his many churches and secular buildings, (refer to Geological map, chapter of Gothic British Isles). Brick, thinner than today, were at first bonded irregularly in loose form of 'English bond', but after mid 17th century; 'Flemish bond' became usual.

·*English Bond - brickwork with alternate courses.

of stretches and headers.

- *Header - a masonry unit laid so that its ends are exposed, two or more adjc;cent withes of masonry and tying them together; a bonder.
- *Stretcher - a masonry unit laid horizontally with its length in the direction of the.face of the wall.
- *stretcher bond - vertical joints of one course falling midway between those of adjacent course.
- *Flemish bond - a bond in which each course consists of headers and stretchers laid alternately, each header·is centred with resi>ect to the stretcher above and stretcher below it.
- *stack bond - a pattern bond the facing brick is laid with all vertical joints continuously aligned.
- *soldier - (a masonry unit which is set on end, with itsface showing on the wall surface.


487

![image 842](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile842.png)

Terra cotta was not much used till the victorian times. Roofing:tiles accompanied the use of bricks in the eastern and southern parts of the country, while stone slates or flags (a hard, brittle, meta-morphic rock used extensively as dimension stone in thin for flooring; roofing, etc.I replaced thatch in the upland midland, western and northern regions distribution of slates was made by the improvement of waterways, roads, and construction of canals. Cast iron as structural material before 1,800 appeare9 as a consequence of the industrial revolution.

*Terra cotta - earth baked (unglazed) or burnt in moulds. For use in construction and decoration, harder in quality than brick.

###### CLIMATIC

In order to admit abundant light, large windows still continued, especially in the early period, in striking contrast to those of Italy. A growing desire for comfort, coinciding also with the more general use of coal as feul in the reign of Charles I, brought about the introduction of a fireplace in each room; while chimneys continued, as in the Tudor period, to be prominent symmetrical, features of the external instead of being disguised as in Italy.

###### ........:·'!;

###### HISTORICAL, SOCIAL AND RELIGIOUS

Henry VIII had been firmly established on the English Throne. His meeting with Francis I, helped to attract foreign artists to his court, and they largely determined the manner of the adoption of the Renaissance style in England, alike in architecture sculpture and painting. Continental Protestantism began winning English converts around 1520. On the failure of the Pope to nullify Henry VIII's marriage, he excluded papal jurisdiction from his kingdom. In

he became supreme head of the English church and dissolved the monasteries and sold a large part of their estates. This ministers of his young son Edward VI protestanized the English church. 1

In 1559 an Anglican church was restored with Queen Mary as its Supreme governor and the Book·of common prayer as its liturgy. The sense of security and prosperity which followed found material expression in the splendid mansion-building of the period. Monasteries fell into ruins, others were incorporated iri the mansions' of the new nobility.

The Stuarts brought England into closer touch with the continent, more especially with France and Italy. Architecture, painting arid the minor crafts were forested by the fine artistic sense of the King, but the civil war arrested progress in architecture. In 1666 the great fire gave Sir Christopher Wren an unparalled opportunity for church-building in London:..._

The first half of the 18th century saw Dutch influence on architecture gradually anglicized and the houses that were now built were of the convenient and comfortable type known Queen Anne and Georgian, well suited to the needs of the increasing middle classes, both in town and country.

Renaissance humanism appealed strongly to a community which had thrown off ecclesiastical domination a·nd was rapidly developing a free national and domestic life along more secular lines. A new class of wealthy merchants a·nd traders required new houses suitable to their health & standing in the country. These were the men who adopted the new style and the printers, Jed by CAXTON, has brought the picture book and printing of details and plans of classic buildings within the reach of the common humanity.

488

![image 843](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile843.png)

Foreign artists, imbued with Renaissance ideas, had already flocked to the court of Henry VIII. The progressive development in domestic comfort and the increase of hospitality during the reign of Elizabeth I1558-1603) were responsible for an era remarkable for the erection of those great and commodious houses which are still the special pride ·of England. It also became fashionable for young men to visit Italy, and thus Renaissace ideas and tastes were brought to England. The period as a whole saw considerable growth of education and of charitable endeavour.

The Bank of England was established in 1694 the economic situation underwent a marked change and the mercantile system secured a surplus of exports over imports, which naturally resulted in an increase of home manufactures. There was a greater sense of security which created better conditions for general architectural enterprise. The general increase in wealth and the rise in the standard comforts are seen in the number of plain comfortable Georgian houses of country towns.

###### ARCHITECTURAL CHARACTER

English Renaissance architecture may be divided as follows:

- 1. Early Renaissance ......................................................
- 2. late Renaissance


Elizabeth (1558-1603) Jacobean (1603-25) Stuart (1625-1702} Georgian (1702-1830)

I. EARLY RENAISSANCE

1. Elizabethan Architecture

The reign of Elizabeth witnessed the establishment of the Renaissance style in England. Elizabethan architecture, which followed the Tudor, was a transition style with Gothic features and Renaissance detail. The Renaissance influences reached· Engla.nd from flanders, which at the time was practicing a decoratively-exaggerated style of 'strap work' and grotesque mainly based on the work of the Italian stuccoists. The zeal for church building in the middle ages in England had provided churches wrnch remained sufficient for popular needs, and thus Elizabethan architecture was secular rather than ecclesiastical in its nature, and was the outcome of the needs of a time when powerful statesmen, successful merchants, and the enriched ,gentry required mansions suitable to their new position·and these were built in England, mainly in the country, in contrast with the churches & palaces ih Italian cities.

These great houses were not designed comprehensively by a single person but were rather the conceptions jointly of the owner and his chosen master-craftsmen (for large buildings, chief was usually a mason) and subordinate specialist craftsmen might be employed to execute decorative features like entrance-doorways, porticoes, fireplaces, staircases and panelling, to their own designs.

The mansions displayed many new combinations of features. Externally, towers, gables, parapets, balustrades and chin:mey stacks produced an effective skyline, and walls were enlivened by oriel and bay-windows with mullions and transoms, while internally the same style, when applied to fittings, furniture, and decoration, made for repose, dignity and uniformity.

Elizabethan mansions looked outwards rather than inwards towards courtyards as in the Mediaeval periods, so that there now could be formal settings related to each front, a forecourt, perhaps with decorative gateway and angle pavilions, on the entrance side, and on the others, formal· gardens with beds of plants, arranged in intricate geometrical knots and a central fountain marking converging paths, or balustraded terraces, topiary gardens and walks, or terraces and orchards.

![image 844](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile844.png)

EXAMPLES OF ELIZABETHAN ARCHITECTURE

a. MONUMENTS, TOMBS AND FITTINGS

Tomb of Henry VII, (1509) in Westminster Abbey is an early and exquisite example of Renaissance art. It is a black marble table tomb, with angle corinthian pilasters, between whith are the royal arms while above are winged cherubs and effigies of Henry VII and his queen, enclosed in a chantry chapel with a fine Gothic screen by Ducheman.

- *cherubs - one of the winged heavenly beings that support the 'throne of God or act as guardian spirits, or chubby, rosy-faced child with wings.


b. ELIZABETHAN MANSIONS

These mansions show a general similarity in their arrangement with those of .the Jacobean period. The smaller mansions had a central hall flanked at one end by kitchen and offices, and at the other by withdrawing and living-rooms; while the larger types was quadrangular with similar accomodation, but with additional rooms grouped round the court, and with a gatehouse in the centre of the entrance side. Elizabethan and Jacobean architects adhered to the Tudor plan for smaller mansions, but they evolved theE-shaped plan from the quadrangular plan by omitting one side of the court, thus admitting sunlight and air.

490

![image 845](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile845.png)

The H-shaped plan was used also in this period. The gatehouse often became a detached building features, sucn as the great hall, grand staircase, and long gallery, are common to the typical houses..As houses began to look outwards instead of into courts, surrounding gardens developed on formal lines.

The GREAT HALL still retained its central position, but became more than ever a hall of state, connecting the various parts of the mansion.

The walls were cased internally in oak panelling to a height of 2.4 m or 3m (8 or 10

. ft.). surmounted by ancestral portraits; armour and trophies of the chase. The fireplace with its huge dog-grate, was an elaborate feature flanked by columns, while above were ranged by heraldic devices of the owners. The hall was covered either by an open timber roof or with elaborately moulded plaster panels.

*heraldic - a coat of arms.

![image 846](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile846.png)

At the entrance end the·carved oak screen supported the minstrel's gallery and also screened off the kitchen department beyond; while at the other end of the hall was the lofty bay window and raised dais, from which were reached the living-rooms of the family. A similar arrangement was adopted in the colleges of oxford, cambridge and Inns of court, London as Gray's in Hall and middle temple Hall.

- *bay window - the window of a protruded bay or the windowed bay itself.
- *dais - a raised platform reserved for the seating of speakers or dignitatires.


###### GRAV''.S INI-J HAL.L: INTERIOR.

492

![image 847](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile847.png)

The GRAND STAIRCASE, with carved newels and pierced balustrades, and usually adjacent to the hall; forms dignified approach to the rooms above, and its prominence as a feature is in marked contrast with the inconvenient corkscrew stairs of the.mediaeval period.

1SHR..OP.&Hltui

The Long Gallery-is perhaps th"e most striking feature of an Elizabethan mansion, with ornamental chimney-pieces, panelled or tapestried walls, large mullioned windows and modelled plaster ceiling. Long, low and narrow, though varied .bY room-like bays.

The gallery often ran the whole length of the upper floor of the house and connected the wings on either side of.the central hall.

493

![image 848](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile848.png)

The Withdrawing-room or 'solar' of previous times was often elaborately finished with carved chimney-pieces and panelled walls. Bedrooms were multiplied and were often elaborate and a private chapel was frequently incorporated in the building.

HardwicK Hall, Derbyshire (1590-7} is unusual in plan consisting of a rectangular block with projecting bays. The exterior is famous for its large mullioned and transomed windows, giving rise to the saying 'Hardwick Hall'. more glass than wall, while bay-windows carried up as towers, relieve the skyline and are terminated by open scroll-work with the initials E.S. for Elizabeth, countess of Shrewsbury, known as 'Bess of Hardwick'.

CASTLE ASHBY, Northants (1 572) situated on high ground, avos originally in the form of a three-sided court, which included the great hall, 18m x 9 m, with screens; bay-window, and staircase turrets. The lettered balustrade displays the words 'Ni.•:i Dominus AEdificaverit. ·

###### 2. Jacobean Architecture

As Roman Literature and models became better known, a subtle change crept in, and the sober regularity of classic columns and entablatures gradually supplanted the irregularity of Elizabethan architecture. There was a greater tendency for new structures to be designed by a single hand. Buildings still continued to be for domestic rather than religious use. ar;td thus the style developed along lines suited to popular needs, w1th considerable latitude indetail and ornament; not only for buildings, but also tor fittings and furniture, which now became. more abundant in quantity and more decorative m quality.

###### EXAMPLES OF JACOBEAN ARCHITECTURE JACOBEAN MANSIONS

a. Hatfield House, Herts (1607-11 I built for Robert, first Earl of Salisbury, stands preeminent among many noble piles of this period in displaying the special characteristics and elaboration of treatment considered suitable for the country mansion man.sion of a nobleman. The house is E-shaped in plan, with central hall and projecting symmetrical wings, and is set off by formal gardens.

The entrance front is 68.5 m long, is of daringly plain brickwork with stone mullioned windows, relieved by a projecting central entrance; while the bay-windows of the wings are taken up as small lateral towers, and the building is finished by a flat roof and balustrade and dominated by a central clock-turret.

The South front is much ornate in treatment, with Doric, Ionic and Corinthian orders superimposed to form a centre-piece flanked by an arcaded ground storey·, mullioned windows and pierced parapet. The two storeyed hall with mullioned windows, minstrels's gallery, and modelled plaster ceiling, is a Renaissance development. The long gallery, chapel, grand staircase and of private rooms all contribute to the completeness of this Jacobean mansion.

![image 851](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile851.png)

![image 852](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile852.png)

b. Bramshill House, Hampshire (1605-12) has an unusual plan, partly due to an older building, is of the H-type, with entrance through an arcaded porch dir.ect into the hall, which thus loses its feudal character, but still retains the dais. An old feature is the long narrow internal area. The long gallery, 39.6 m long, and the terrace with its arcades and the oriel window are among the beautiful features of this building.

..Oriel - a bay window corbeled out from the wall of an upper storey, a bay projecting inside or out, extending a room. Or a windowed bay or porch at the top of exterior stairs.

###### JACOBEAN COLLEGES

a. The Bodleian Library, Oxford (1613-36) is a conspicuous instance ot the work of ·

the period, for the tower over the gateway is a curious but effective mixture of traditional Gothich and new Renaissance, with mullioned windows and canopied niches flanked by the fine orders of architecture, one above the other; while the whole is capped by Gothic pinnacles.

II. LATE RENAISSANCE

1. STUART Architecture (1625-1702)

a. Banqueting House, London (1619-22) was erected by ll.igo Jones. The complete plan of the palace with its seven courts, shows the position the Banqueting, House would have occupied on the Grand court 243 m x 122m t_wice the size of the court of the Louvre, Paris. The two storeys are enriched with superimposed corinthian and Ionic orders fully modelled about the three central bays. There is a rustic basement and an upper frieze of festoons and masks. The pilasters are doubled at the angles and the walls are rusticated behind the plain shafts of the orders.

Originally the windows were· of the 'croisee' leaded type set in alternating triangular and segmental pedimented frames, the upper tier having straight cornices. Designed for the performance of court masques, all but the end and the centre .windows were originally dummies.

..cro1see. - (Ft. crossing) transept; the French term for the type of casement window preferred for the last three centuries in France.

###### PL.AN

The interior has a length of 141 m including pase, a breadth including aisles of 30.8 m and an area of about 6.000 m2. This plan consists of a great central space at the crossing suitable for vast congregatrons, crowned by a Dome painted by Sir James Thornhill; a choir and nave .in three bays, north and south transepts with semicircular porticoes, and projecting western portico of coupled columns.·

The Dome and its support presented a complicated structural problem. The Dome is carried on eight piers, and is 34·m i(l diameter at the base of the high drum, and is of triple construction. The inner dome of brick, 457 mm (18 ins.) thick has its eye 65.3 m. above the ffoor, while the intermediate conical dome. of brick 457 mm (18 ins.} thick, strengthened by a double of Iron supports the stone lantern, ball and cross. Eight openings are formed in the summit of the outer dome to admit light to the inner dome.

The facades have two orders, the lower corinthian and the upper composite. totall· ing 33.6 m in height. The aisles are only one storey high, so the part above them is a screen-wall introduced to give dignity and to .act as a counter weight to the flying buttresses concealed behind it. which receive the thrust of the nave vault. The western facade. 54 m wide, approached by a broad flight of steps which give scale to the building, has a central two-storeyed portico of coupled cori!lthian and composite columns superimposed, surmounted by a pediment sculptured with the conversion of St. Paul. The portico is flanked by two beautifully proportioned cam· panile 64.7 m high above the nave floor, that on the north containing bells and that on the south the clock.

![image 865](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile865.png)

2. Georgian Architecture (1702-1830)

HOUSES- The Georgian house was generally planned as a simple symmetrical square or rectangular block with or without wings. The grander winged examples were the seats of the more well-to-do and the aristocracy. The centre.third of the main block in both types was usually occupied by the entrance hall, which, in the larger houses was carried up the full height of two storeys. The staircase, also designed for show, was either axially beyond or to one side of the hall and was seen through columned or arched screens. In the simple block type a single staircase sufficed, but in winged mansions, two staircases became necessary on account of the high ha11.

Communication was then achieved at first-floor level by a gallery. Winged types often had light wells. The precedent for this type was stoke Bruerne Park, Northants.

In the simple block type !he ancillary rooms were grouped round the hall and stair nucleus within the compact rectangular envelope. The kitchen, stores and offices

relegated to the basement.

EXAMPLES of the simple block plan:

The SWAN HOUSE, Chichester (1711) is built wholly in gauged and rubbed brickwork, the centre third thrust forward to frame narrow lights on either side of the pilastered and segmentally pedimented door case. Above the dentil and moulded brick cornice is a panelled parapet partly concealing the hipped and tilled roof. Exposed sash boxes and thick glazing bars enliven the front. As this is a town house, it is set back from the pavement edge and ha.s a wrought-iron balustrade on a low wall with gate piers and steps.

507

![image 866](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile866.png)

The Moot house, Downton Wiltshire (1650) - has flemish bond brick elevation with stone dressings, the centre projecting to form a tall, narrow, pedimented feature. It has a hipped roof with hipped dormers, and a moulded and modillioned eaves cornice and·alternate Quoins. The sash boxes are exposed to view, with thick glazing bars, and there is a pedimented door case.

508

![image 867](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile867.png)

EXAMPLES of the central block with wings:

Blenheim Palace, Oxfordshlre (1704-20) is the most monumental mansion in England. Sir John Vanbrugh's bold and forceful personality stamped itself on all his designs which are characterized by his love of projecting porticos, twined columns supports for trophies and the contrast of light and shade.

Designed by William Kent a representative of Palladian character in the present class. The plan shows no deliberate framing of the entrance court by the wings, which here number four instead of two and give symmetrical elevations on all four fronts. A grand feature of the central block is the halt, adorned by Ionic, colonnades which turn in an apse to enclose an axial flight of steps rising the 'piano nobile' or principal floor; the chief apartments include a stately gallery of three related rooms. Externally. on the south front there is the inevitable Palladian Portico, while the angles of the main block are raised by attic storeys into pavilions, each of which.has a chief feature a 'Venetian Window', <;>f a type driving from the so-called palladian motif.

###### RENAISSANCE ARCHITECTURE in GERMANY AND CENTRAL EUROPE 16th-19th Century

###### INFLUENCES

###### GEOGRAPHICAL

By reason of their central position in Europe the teutonic peoples absorbed Renaissance art from Italy to the South and from France to the west, but the distan·ce from the centre of the movement deferred its arrival until some 125 years after its emergence in Italy. The states were distinguished by different geographical conditions, and these differences were reflected in the architecture.

![image 871](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile871.png)

CENTRAL EUROPE IN RENAISSANCE PERIOD

###### GEOLOGICAL

Timber, brick and stone continued to impart their character to architecture, according to local usage. Moulded and ornamental brickwork was used in great variety in th·e northern alluvial plains. While stone and timber were handled in the vernacular manner of the locality.

###### CLIMATIC

The revived classic forms were modified from those of Italy to sait a more northern temperature. Thus windows continued ·to be large, roofs to be steep to throw off snow, and chimneys, necessary for heating, to be prominent features.

512

![image 872](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile872.png)

###### HISTORICAL, SOCIAL AND RELIGIOUS

The succession of Charles V to the possessions of the houses of casti.le, Aragon and Burgundy, marks the climax of the German Renaissance. VaFious invasions by the Turks between the years 1529and 1562further complicated matters in Germany, increased the difficulties of the House of Hapsburg, and were iminical tcrarchitectural activities. The wars of Charles V and the catholics against the protestant princes 11547-55) were brought to an end by the Peace Qf Augsburg, which allowed each state to set up what religion it pleased, and to exile individuals who were of different religion from that of prtwailing government.

The growing maladjustment and the political incapacity of both sides culminated in the famous 'Thirty year$· ·was (1618-48} between catholic·and Protestant Princes. These wars not only arrested the development of architecture during the period of their actual prosecution, but also retarded building activities for sometime after the con.clusion of peace.

Renaissance Germany was composed of divers margravates, palatinates, electorates, duchies, ecclesiastical states and imperial cities, subject to different reigning houses of Hapsburg, Hohenzollern, Wittelsbach and Wettin. There obtained a great diversity in artistic development. The Holy Roman Empire still failed to function effectively, even though in 1556 it was separa·ted from Spain and assigned to Ferdinand. Feudalism began to disappear; gunpower changed military methods and bands of mercenaries replaced feudal troops. The principal Renaissance factor was the influence.of the universitities, notably of Heidelberg, the chief seat of the Humanist movement. This was further strengthened by the invention of printing, while in the 18th century, the literary works of winckelmann, Goethe and others

. aroused interest in the architecture of ancient

Martin Luther (1483-1546) to..yers above all other Germans of his time as the dominating

fiaure of the reformation in Germany, and the day in 1517 on which hepublished in Wit-

tenberg his famous thesis against indulgences inaugurated a revolution in the religious life of Germany. He subsequently burnt bull of excommunication issued against him by the Pope, winning support from the rulers of Saxony, Hesse anc;t numerous German rulers. A decree of the Diet of Speyer (1529) forbidding ecclesiastical changes, called forth the protest from Luther and his adherents which originated the name of Protestant. The stress and turmoil in religious thought of this period of upheaval allowed little opportunity for the erection of new churches, but it resulted in the -transformation of those of previous periods to meet the needs of the reformed religion, in the ritual of which preaching became a powerful factor, and necessitated that increased space for seated congregations which brought about the in· traduction of galleries. The strife between protestant and catholics was followed by the counter-reformation! reinforced by the arrival of the Jesuits and the council of trent (1563).

*gallery - a communicating passage or wide

· corridor for pictures and statues. Ari upper storey for seats in a church .

513

![image 873](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile873.png)

###### ARCHITECTURAL CHARACTER

The Renaissance style appeared in Germany about 1,550. Approximately, the successive periods were:

- a. -Early Renaissance (1550-1600) chiefly consisting of the introduction of Renaissance elements into Gothic buildings or of additions to them.
- b. Proto-Baroque (1600-60) in which Italian themselves carried the Renaissance from north lta_ly to Switzerland, Austria and Germany, while native architects began successfully to emulate them and produce national versions of the style.
- c. Baroque (1600-1710), in which architects principally of native origin but who often had received part of their training in Rome or elsewhere in Italy, brought German architecture to a splendled culimination;
- d. Rococo (1710-60), an extension of the Baro(lue period wherein architecture and decoration show great refinement and technical mastery but less vigour and force.
- e. Antiquarian (1760-1830), in which there is a progressive return to ancient classical models, the Greek revival being a manifestation and somewhat beyond the period 1790-1830.


Broadly speaking the greatest works of the German Renaissance date from the hun.dred years 1660-1760, the Baroqu_e suited the people, peasant and patrician alike, in its intensely visual appeal; the rich profusion of ornamentation in chu.rch interiors was not wilful display but told the Bible story much as hieroglyphs, moral reliefs or stained glass had carried the religious message in other times and places.

Church and palace interiors may sometimes appear over-ornate, even gaudy, but the opulent magnificence that frequ_ently resulted was a direct response to social, religious and political circumstances, and just as spontaneous as the gaiety universally manifested in peasant art. Ornamentation was deliberate and purposeful, and had a greater part in ecclesiastical, particularly Jesuit, buildings than in secular architecture, which was relatively plain, except in the case of the mansionsand country residences of the nobility, where it was appropriate to express gr-acious living.

###### EXAMPLES

###### 1. Secular Architecture

1. Heidelberg Csstle (1531-1612) -welt exemplifies progressive developments of the Early Renaissance in the various additions to the Mediaeval castle. The later of them suffer from over-ornamentation. There is a great watch tower and an irregular court around which are grouped the Renaissance buildings.

The Saalbau, the northeast corner shows Gothic features mingled with those of the incoming Renaissance: This is relatively plain. The Heinrichsbau, has superimposed tonic and Corinthian pilasters and half columns, two light windows showing venetian affinities, and symbolic statues in 'niches'.

###### RENAISSANCE ARCHITECTURE RUSSIA 16th-19th Century

###### INFLUENCES

GEOGRAPHICAL

West of the Urals, Russia covers 2,100,000 square miles (5,500,000 km2). In a land of such enormous distances, and with a highly centralized government, Renaissance architecture was established in the capital city as an importation from abroad. Provincial buildings then followed suit.

###### GEOLOGICAL

Building stone has never been a feature of Russian architecture. The forest of the north provided ample supplied· of wood, which was in almost universal use for smaller domestic buildings and influenced the designed churches. But throughout the vast plain which is Russia proper, the main material of Renaissance buildings is brick.

###### CLIMATIC

The severe northern climate called for high-pitched roofs and the onion domes on buildings which are indigenous to the country.

###### HISTORICAL & SOCIAL

The history of Russian architecture is not one of continuous development, but of separate phases, each resulting from political events and associated with the arrival of foreign architects on Russian soil. Early mediaeval church architecture derives from Byzantine culture, which was brought to Kiev with orthodox christianity in the 10th century.

In the third decade of the 13th century Russia was overrun by the tartars and cut off from the rest .of Europe, with an almost complete cersation of artistic activity for a century and a half. When the new Russia emerged, centred on Moscow, Ivan Ill (1462-1505) the ruler to take the title of Tsar, Sent for Italian architects to reconstruct the Kremlin.

A third dramatic change of direction occured with the founding of St. Petersburg by Peter the great in 1703, and the construction of his new capital as a window on the west. This 3rd period merits more recognition that it usually receives, as a vigor achievement of Baroque and Classicism on a scale unequalled in any other city.

528

![image 893](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile893.png)

###### ARCHITECTURAL CHARACTER

The brick walls and towers of the Moscow Kremlin, and the Cathedral of the Dormition {the coronation church of the Tsars) were built by Italian architects in the last quarter of the 15th centu,Y, but deliberately related to medieval Russian prototypes. In the 16th century, side by side with the many domes of monastic foundations, a new type of Votive church appeared in the form of an Octagonal tower crowned with a tent-shaped roof. Bar<)que ornament applied in contrasting colour to these varied forms produced a picturesque blend of Russian and western architecture, the 'Moscow' or 'Naryshkin' Baroque.

St. Petersburg, the t1rst great City ot mooern ttmes to oe ouut to a pre-determined plan, reflects the taste of Peter the Great and his successors, and their choice or architects. Although largely by foreigners, the buildings show a vastness of scale, an exuberance and use of colour which are essentially Russian. Secular architecture is4)redominant, and the granite quays of the neva, the intersecting canals and the immense squares are lined with great palaces and public buildings of brick, plastered and painted yellow, blue or green, to offset the white of classical orders and decoration.

The design of all buildings was controllea and none were allowed higher than the winter palace, except for the occasional gilt needle-spire or church dome punctuating the skyline. Such interiors as have survived are respondent with glittering chandeliers and marble col-umns,

529

![image 894](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile894.png)

MOSCOW-The church of the Intercession of the virgin (16931 at FilL Basically a churcli of the 16th century pyramidal type to which Ukrainian

. ornament is applied. The subtleties of

qu.e three-dimensional design appear in the upward progression through a cube with apsidal projections on fqu.r side&, a simple cube, and the octagons diminishing in size. The walls are of pink bricks. with coJonnettes, window surrounds and parapets of white stone, a bulbous gift cupola at the summit, and four others at mid-height.

Leningrad

St. Petersburg (1747-8) by Rastrelli, demonstrates to the full his success in applying three-dimensional.

forms to the needs of the Orthodox church.

The Ekaterininsky {Catherine) palaca (1749-56) is no less than 298m in length. Above a basement storey, the giant Corinthian order of the principal and attic storeys carries a balustrade and broken pediments. The facade is broken down into sections. with groups of columns supported by caryatids, and varied treatment of windows. The huge scale the white columns and blue walls, the gult domes of the chapel, are all unmistakeably Russian.

RENAISSANCE ARCHITECTURE in BELGIUM AND THE NETHERLANDS

###### 16th-19th Century

###### INFLUENCES

###### GEOGRAPHICAL

The Netherlands is a term formerly embraced the whole of the 'Nederland' tHollandl and Belgium. The movement of power and commerce from the mediterranean to the Atlantic and Northern sea of Europe placed the low countries in a favourabJe central position 'to share in the new sea power, trade, world exploration and colonial expansion; at the sametime they were directly in the path of conflicts between the major powers.

The period opens with Antwerp.! After the establishment of the Dutch republic ( 1588! the ports of Zealand, the Zuider Zee, and especially of Amsterdam and Rotterdam, rose to supremacy. The Dutch develpped as an energetic and seafaring nation. In the 17th century, the Dutch began their great engineering feats of draining and reclaiming land, of building polders. dykes and canals. of which they have long been famous.

![image 899](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile899.png)

Groningen•

Harletfl

NORTH 5£A

###### i

i

###### THE NETHERLANDS IN THE SEVENTEETH CENTURY

534

![image 900](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile900.png)

###### GEOLOGICAL

Stone, brick and marbles continued to be the chief building materials of Belgium, together with the timber of the Ardennes. In Holland, bricks made _from her clay soil created her · relatively sober national architecture.

###### CLIMATIC

There are two main climatic zones in the low countries, the one relating to the_l highlands of eastern Belgium and Dutch Limburg, and the other to the low-lying parts of the Flemish and Dutch coastal areas. The architecture of Holland is greatly influenced by its sea-girt, river intersected and low-lying, ten character, where the frequently driving rains, the winds and clear light produced a simplicity of facade and large windows, with c9mpositions conceived in terms of planes rather than sculptural form. Such circumstances apply less strongly to lowland Belgium.

###### HISTORICAL, SOCIAL AND RELIGIOUS

The 17th century in Belgium was not a peaceful one, and, with. the decay of Antwerp's trade her prosperity suffered greatly. In the 18th century, Spanish authority was followed by a period of French rule (1700-6) and under the treaty of Utrecht (1713) the country passed to Australia. Though equally plagued by wars in the 17th century, the Dutch Republic, dominated by the rich and highly urbanized province of Holland, became a great naval power and maritime trading nation with overseas colonies.

The Kingdom of the Netherlands, including Belgium, Holla'1d and Luxembourg created in 1815 with Wiltiam I of orange as its head. The rule of the Dutch king was unwise, and situation so disadvantageous to the Belgians·that they revolted and became a separate Kingdom in 1830.

As the properity of the South waned, the fortunes of the North greew and reached a climax at the 'Golden' 17th century, with the Dutch Republic as a great European power. The power of wealth through trade was largely in the hands of the burgher class, principally of the pr9vinces of Holland and Zealand·, within the semi-monarchial Republic, the princess of Orange has a court which increasingly looked to France for a lead i(l taste and fashions. The burghers followed the court in its patronage of arts, and their houses were richly decorated and furnished, giving a lively. Market for the many artists of this great period of Dutch art.

The beliefs of Luther and Calvin were received early in the low countries, but since Charles V and Philipp II rulers during the greater part of the 16th century, regarded the catholic church and the state as indivisible, Protestants were presecuted. However, the revolt and war against the Spaniards became more than religious: Indeed the Protestant Prince of Orange led armies which included a minority of catholics.

The Spanish Netherlands (lat'er Belgium} remained almost wholly catholic and followed the lead of Rome in church building, wherein as elseWhere, the Baroque style was favoured by the Jesuits. The Dutch, after first adapting and even copying the old churches and setting and enormous pulpit halfway along th.e side of the nave, arranging 'pews to face it, ex-

with a variety o.f central plans which were developed to suit the Refc;mned religion and taste.

###### 535

![image 901](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile901.png)

###### ARCHITECTURAL CHARACTER

The Early Renaissance became notable in 1515. Its strong individually is mainly due national conditions and characteristics. It is rich externally and internally, is rarely grand in scale and, due to the northern climate windows in domestic work are even larger than the French and may occupy almost as much space as wall. Architectural details prdgressivefy assume a more authentic Italian character, but methods of composition are in a variety ot ways unique. The .Early Renaissance of Belgium continued until the end of the 16th century.

For the 17th century, it is essential to speak separately of Belgium and Holland. Belgium progressed with its individualistic interpretation of the Italian Pro-Baroque flourishing at that time, to a style which may be described as Baroque. In the century, it shows in its decoration the influence of Austrian and French 'Rococo, while from the mid-century it toQk the Antiquarian turn. Towards the end of the same century, occasional Greek revival manifestations appeared.

Ducth architecture took a substantially different course. Lieven de Key and Hendrik de Keyser (1565-1621 l developed the early Dutch style. usually plainer than the Belgian. It matured a Palladian phase of considerable dignity and quality in 1625 to 1670, where positive. austerity was practiced wherein external decoration being almost wholly excluded. Next, Daniel Mar.ot a Huguenot refugee, introduced the masculine style of Louis XIV to the Dutch court, effective in influencing interior decoration rather than architecture proper, and t ·:enceforward French fashions continued to be followed, though with sober external expression and bold and effective planning.

EXAMPLES

I. SECULAR ARCHITECTURE

A. BELGIAN

1. The Chancellery, Bruges (1535) a town house has.seemingly precocious Baroque qualities which are in fact due to the perpetuation of the flamboyant Gothic spirit. It has a two-storeyed facade with quasi-Doric orders, mullioned and transomed windows, and central gable with side-scrolls, crocl<ets and figures.

536

![image 902](George Salvan Architectural Character and the History of Architecture_images/imageFile902.png)

2. The Mussee' Plantin-Moretus, Antwerp (1550) is more authentically classical, and its extent and character illustrates the opulence and social status of a highly successful flemish burgher.

###### ...,....... Ml.ISES' Pl...AN"TINM.OJ!.E!TUS,

3. The Town Hall, Antwerp (1561 -6) is an imporant prototype of Belgian Early Renaissance. It has superimposed orders between closely-spaced large windows, a rustical basement storey and a galleried upper storey. Its centre-piece is highly characteristic of the phase, with its freely manipulated classical detail and generous use of plastic ornament.

537

2. The Royal Palace, Amsterdam (1648-65) is a major example of Dutch civic architecture on an unusually large scale. Its style is palladian in the sense that it is of clear simple ordinance with no important departures from classical rule and that there is no intrusion of ornament on to the architectural lines. There is however, a greater freedom in the design of an open cupola-turret over the central, shallowly-projecting pavilion of the two-tiered pilastered facade, standing on a low basement storey and the crowning pediment has an infilling of petty sculpture.

In its west facade, with its superimposed Ionic and composite columns, broken pediments and enormous side scrolls masking the aisle roofs, St. Michel shows a close approach to the Baroque of Rome, except that its portal is mean and less focal than it would be there, while the banded orders show some French influence and the·ornament· has native Netherlands, characteristics.
